\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\big\|\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}-\frac{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}{\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|}\big\|\leq\varepsilon
.

Proof.

We prove by induction on the dimension
d
that for every
\delta\in(0,2)
, with
d\log\frac{8}{\delta}
inner product sign queries we can obtain a vector
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}
such that
\big\|\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}-\frac{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}{\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|}\big\|\leq 2d\delta
; the lemma then follows by setting
\delta=\frac{\varepsilon}{2d}
.

Just one query suffices when
d=1
, so consider the base case
d=2
. Perform a binary search as follows: Start with the cone
K_{0}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with corresponding angle
2\pi
. In iteration
t
, we maintain a cone
K_{t}
containing
\textstyle\bf g
whose angle is half that of
K_{t-1}
as follows. For each iteration, find a line
\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}:\langle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\rangle=0\}
that cuts
K_{t}
into two cones
K_{t}^{L}=K_{t}\cap\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}:\langle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\rangle\leq 0\}
and
K_{t}^{R}=K_{t}\cap\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}:\langle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\rangle\geq 0\}
each with half the angle of
K_{t}
, i.e. bisecting
K_{t}
. Ask the query “Is
\langle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\rangle\geq 0
?”, and if so set
K_{t+1}=K_{t}^{R}
, otherwise set to
K_{t+1}=K_{t}^{L}
, and repeat the procedure. By construction all the cones
K_{t}
contain
\textstyle\bf g
, and after
\log\frac{8}{\delta}
iterations we obtain a cone
K
with angle
\frac{\delta\pi}{4}
. Let
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}
be any vector in this cone with unit
\ell_{2}
-norm. For any other
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\in K
also of unit norm, we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\|_{2}^{2}=2-2\langle\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\rangle\leq 2-2\cos(\delta\pi/4)\leq(\delta\pi/4)^{2}\leq\delta^{2},

where the second inequality uses that fact that
\cos(\theta)\geq 1-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}
for all
\theta\in(0,\pi/2)
. So
\|\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\|_{2}\leq\delta
for all unit-norm vectors in
K
, and in particular
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}
gives the desired approximation of
\frac{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}{\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|_{2}}
, proving the desired result when
d=2
.

Now consider the general case
d>2
. Consider any 2-dimensional subspace
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
\Pi_{A}
denote the projection onto this subspace. Using the
2
-dimensional case on the subspace
A
, we see that by using
\log\frac{8}{\delta}
queries of the form “Is
\langle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}},\Pi_{A}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\rangle\geq 0
?”, we can obtain a unit length vector
\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\in A
such that
\|\lambda_{A}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}-\Pi_{A}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\leq\delta\|\Pi_{A}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
, where
\lambda_{A}:=\|\Pi_{A}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
.
We note that since
\langle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}},\Pi_{A}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\rangle=\langle\Pi_{A}^{*}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\rangle
, the required queries can be obtained by inner product sign queries (here
\Pi_{A}^{*}
denotes the adjoint linear operator for the projection operator
\Pi_{A}
, whose matrix representation is given by the transpose of the matrix representing the projection
\Pi_{A}
).

Now consider the
(d-1)
-dimensional subspace
B:=\textup{span}\{\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}},A^{\perp}\}
, and notice that
\dist({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}},B)\leq\delta\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
: the vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}:=\lambda_{A}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}+({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}-\Pi_{A}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}})
belongs to
B
and
\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\|=\|\lambda_{A}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}-\Pi_{A}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\leq\delta\|\Pi_{A}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\leq\delta\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
. Since
\textstyle\bf g
is close to this subspace, we project it there and recurse on dimension. More precisely, consider the projection
\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}
of
\textstyle\bf g
onto
B
, and inductively obtain a vector
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\in B
such that
\|\lambda_{B}\cdot\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}-\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|_{2}\leq 2(d-1)\delta\cdot\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
(letting
\lambda_{B}:=\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
), by using additional
(d-1)\log\frac{8}{\delta}
queries (for a total of
d\log\frac{8}{\delta}
queries).

We claim that
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}
is the desired approximation of
\textstyle\bf g
, namely
\|\frac{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}{\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|}-\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\|_{2}\leq 2d\delta
. To see this, from triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\big\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}-\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\cdot\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\big\|\leq\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}-\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|+\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}-\lambda_{B}\cdot\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\|+\|\lambda_{B}\cdot\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}-\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\cdot\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\big\|.

(16)

The first term of the right-hand side equals
\dist({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}},B)
, which is at most
\delta\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
as argued above. For the second term, by induction we have

\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}-\lambda_{B}\cdot\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\|_{2}\leq 2(d-1)\delta\cdot\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\leq 2(d-1)\delta\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|.

Finally, we claim that the last term of (
16
) is at most
\delta\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
: since
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}
has unit norm, it equals
|\lambda_{B}-\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\||=|\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|-\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\||=\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|-\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
, and by triangle inequality we have

\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\leq\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|+\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}-\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\leq\|\Pi_{B}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|+\delta\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|,

giving the claim.

Applying all these bounds to (
16
), we get that
\big\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}-\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|\cdot\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}\big\|\leq 2d\delta\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}\|
, as desired. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Now we are ready to finish the proof. To obtain an
\varepsilon
-approximate value comparison oracle using
\mathcal{H}^{\textup{dir}}
, we can do a binary search on the function values using the queries
h^{\textup{val}}_{u,c}
with
u=1
and different values of
c
(as the midpoint of the interval in the binary search) – see Definition
5
. Thus, with
O(\log\frac{U}{\varepsilon})
queries, we can implement an
\varepsilon
-approximate value comparison oracle.

For the
\varepsilon
-approximate separation, we apply Lemma
30
above to the separation oracle
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sep}}_{\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}}(f,C)
, with
O\big((n+d)\log\frac{(n+d)R}{\varepsilon}\big)
inner product sign queries to obtain a vector
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}^{\textup{sep}}_{\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}}(f,C)
such that
\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sep}}_{\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}}(f,C)-\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}^{\textup{sep}}_{\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}}(f,C)\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2R\sqrt{n+d}}
(recall the non-zero separation oracles are assumed to have unit length). Using the same arguments as in inequalities (
13
)-(
15
), we see that
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}^{\textup{sep}}_{\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}}(f,C)
gives a cut valid for
C_{-\varepsilon}
, and hence is an
\varepsilon
-approximate separation oracle.

For the
\varepsilon
-approximate value cut oracle, we do the same thing, but apply Lemma
30
to
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sub}}_{\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}}(f,C)
, with
O\left((n+d)\log\left(\frac{(n+d)MR}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)
oracle calls to obtain an approximation
\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sub}}_{\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}}(f,C)-\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}}^{\textup{sub}}_{\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}}(f,C)\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2MR\sqrt{n+d}}
and then use the argument from (
12
).

Remark 31
.

Notice that the main ingredient for implementing approximate oracles using inner product sign queries is to use such queries for approximating a given vector
\textstyle\bf g
(Lemma
30
). We remark that this task is related to (actively) learning a linear classifier, namely that whose normal is given by
\textstyle\bf g
. While there are existing procedures for doing this, they only guarantee the desired approximation with high probability (albeit on a slightly weaker query model), instead of with probability 1 as we want here; see for example
[
1
]
. While it is possible that these methods can be adapted to our setting, we present a different, self-contained statement and proof.

5
Proofs of Theorem
12
and Corollary
13

Proof of Theorem
12
.

Suppose we have an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that reports an
\varepsilon
-solution to any instance in
\mathcal{I}_{n,d,R,\rho,M}
after
u
queries to a full-information first-order oracle based on the first-order chart
\mathcal{G}
, a finite set of instances
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{n,d,R,\rho,M}
, and a true (unknown) instance
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Our goal is to report a feasible
\varepsilon
-solution using few queries to
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
, where
\mathcal{H}
contains all binary queries. For this, we design a procedure that maintains a family
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
of the instances, which always includes the true instance
I
, and possibly determines exact information to pass to
\mathcal{A}
. We will show that we can always either reduce
|\mathcal{U}|
by a constant factor, or determine exact information to use with
\mathcal{A}
.

Denote by
D
the query strategy of
\mathcal{A}
. Initialize
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{I}
, and (ordered) lists
Q=\emptyset,H=\emptyset
, which will serve as query-response pairs for the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
. In particular,
H
will contain full first-order information about the true instance, and
Q
will be the sequence of queries
\mathcal{A}
makes. While
|\mathcal{U}|>1
and
|Q|\leq u
, do the following:

•

Set
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}=D(Q,H)
, and query whether
\textstyle\bf z
is feasible. Let us simply write
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I)
to mean
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sep}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I)
if
\textstyle\bf z
is infeasible for the instance
I
, and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{val}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I)
or
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sub}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I)
if feasible, where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sep}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{val}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sub}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}
are the first-order maps for separating hyperplane, function value and subgradient, respectively, used by the general binary oracle at
\textstyle\bf z
. Write
V_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}
to be the appropriate codomain.

•

Case 1:
For every
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in V_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}

, at most half of the instances
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
give
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I^{\prime})={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}
. Then there exists a set
A\subseteq V_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}
such that the number of instances
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
with
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I^{\prime})\in A
is between
\frac{1}{4}|\mathcal{U}|
and
\frac{3}{4}|\mathcal{U}|
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{0}:=\{I\in\mathcal{U}:{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I^{\prime})\not\in A\}
,
\mathcal{U}_{1}:=\{I\in\mathcal{U}:{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I^{\prime})\in A\}
; thus,
|\mathcal{U}_{i}|\leq\frac{3}{4}|\mathcal{U}|
for
i=0,1
. Query whether the true instance
I
has
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I)\in A
, using the binary query
h_{A}:h_{A}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})=1
iff
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in A
, so that
h_{A}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}(I))=0
if
I\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
and
h_{A}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}(I))=1
if
I\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
.
Update
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{q}
, where
q
is the answer to the query
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}},h_{A})
given by the oracle.

•

Case 2:
There exists
\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}}\in V_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}
such that more than half of the instances
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
have
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I^{\prime})=\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}}
. Query whether the true instance
I
has
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I)=\bar{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}
, using the binary query
h:h(\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}})=1
and
h({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}})=0
for all other inputs
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\not=\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}}
, so that
h({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}(I))=1
iff
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}}(I)=\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}}
.
If
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I)\neq\bar{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}
, then update
\mathcal{U}
by removing from it all instances
I^{\prime}
such that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I^{\prime})=\bar{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}
, reducing the size of
\mathcal{U}
by at least half. Otherwise, if
\textstyle\bf z
was infeasible, we then know the exact separating hyperplane (or function value or subgradient, in the case
\textstyle\bf z
is feasible) for the true instance
I
and the first-order chart
\mathcal{G}
, namely
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf g$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf g$}}}^{\textup{sep}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}(I)=\bar{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}
, and so employ it to update
Q
and
H
by appending
\textstyle\bf z
and
\textstyle\bf v
to them, respectively, which will serve as information for the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
.

In each step, either the size of
\mathcal{U}
decreases by at least
1/4
, or full (exact) first-order information at the query point determined by the query strategy of
\mathcal{A}
is obtained and
Q,H
are updated. The former can only happen
O(\log|\mathcal{I}|)
times until
\mathcal{U}
becomes a singleton, in which case we know the true instance and can report its optimal solution, while if the latter happens
u
times, one can run the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with the information
(Q,H)
to report an
\varepsilon
-approximate solution to the true instance
I
, noting that since the points in
Q
were determined according to the query strategy of the algorithm, the information in
(Q,H)
is indeed sufficient to run the
\mathcal{A}
on for
u
iterations. Hence, after at most
\log|\mathcal{I}|+u
queries to the general binary oracle, one can report an
\varepsilon
-solution to the true instance.∎

Corollary
13
follows immediately when one uses the centerpoint-based algorithm of
[
13
,
3
]
as
\mathcal{A}
, which is the exact oracle version of Algorithm
1
above and needs at most

O\left(2^{n}\,d\,(n+d)\log\left(\frac{dMR}{\min\{\rho,1\}\varepsilon}\right)\right)

queries in the mixed-integer case, or

O\left(d\log\left(\frac{MR}{\min\{\rho,1\}\varepsilon}\right)\right)

queries in the continuous (
n=0
) case to produce an
\varepsilon
-approximate solution to any instance in
\mathcal{I}_{n,d,\rho,M,R}
.

6
Statements and Declarations

Competing Interests.

There are no financial or non-financial interests that are directly or indirectly related to this work.

Appendix A
Information Games

In this section we define the game-theoretic perspective for information complexity and prove Lemma
15
, which we restate for convenience.

See
15

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a family of optimization instances in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\mathcal{O}
be an oracle for
\mathcal{I}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. We define the
information game for
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{O},\varepsilon)
as a two player game between
\alg
and
\adv
. The players make moves alternatingly with
\alg
moving first. For
\alg
, a move is a choice of query
q
from the oracle
\mathcal{O}
. For
\adv
, a move is a choice of
r\in H
, where
H
is the response set of
q
from the immediately preceding move by
\alg
. The only constraint on
\adv
is that there should exist at least one instance in
\mathcal{I}
that will give the same responses as the moves made by
\adv
in the game so far to queries corresponding to the moves made by
\alg
so far. More precisely, at any stage of the game, there must exist an instance
I
such that for every response
r
given by
\adv
at any round of the game played so far to an immediately preceding query
q
made by
\alg
, we have
r=q(I)
.

Notice that a game strategy for
\alg
is equivalent to a query strategy from Definition
2
. Also, any instance
\bar{I}\in\mathcal{I}
gives a game strategy for
\adv
, by simply reporting the response
q(\bar{I})
for all queries
q
made by
\alg
.

In the game tree defined by the above game, a node is said to be
\varepsilon
-unambiguous
if the instances that are consistent with
\adv
’s moves all have a common
\varepsilon
-approximate solution; otherwise, the node is
\varepsilon
-ambiguous
. The game stops when the game reaches an
\varepsilon
-unambiguous node; thus, all leaf nodes of the game tree are
\varepsilon
-unambiguous. When the game stops at an
\varepsilon
-unambigious node
L
, the
loss (or payoff)
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(L)
is defined as the number of moves made by
\alg
to arrive at
L
in the game tree, i.e., it is exactly half of the depth of
L
in the game tree.

A subtree
T
of the game tree is said to be a
full subgame tree
if it is precisely the set of all descendants of a node in the game tree (including itself). A subtree
T
is said to have
finite horizon
if it has bounded depth, i.e., there exists
D>0
such that all nodes of
T
have depth (in
T
) bounded by
D
. Otherwise,
T
is said to have
infinite horizon
(note this includes the case where
T
has an infinite length path and the case where all paths are finite length, but there is no upper bound on the lengths).

We define the value
v(T)
of a finite horizon subtree
T
by induction on the depth of
T
, which we call the
value of the subgame defined by
T
.

•

If
T
consists of a single node
N
,
v(T):=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(N)
if
N
is an
\varepsilon
-unambiguous node, else
v(T):=\infty
.

•

If
T
is rooted at a node
N
corresponding to
\alg
, then
v(T):=\inf\{v(T^{\prime}):T^{\prime}\textrm{ child subtree in }T\textrm{ at }N\}
.

•

If
T
is rooted at a node
N
corresponding to
\adv
, then
v(T):=\sup\{v(T^{\prime}):T^{\prime}\textrm{ child subtree in }T\textrm{ at }N\}
.

Let
T
and
T^{\prime}
be subtrees of the game tree.
T^{\prime}
is said to be a
(finite horizon) truncation of
T
if there exists
D>0
such that
T^{\prime}
consists of all nodes of
T
with depth bounded by
D
. Observe that if
T,T^{\prime}
are finite horizon subtrees and
T^{\prime}
is a truncation of
T
, then
v(T^{\prime})\geq v(T)
. We can thus naturally extend the notion of value to an infinite horizon subtree
T
, as a limit of its finite horizon truncations:

v(T):=\inf\{v(T^{\prime}):T^{\prime}\textrm{ finite horizon truncation of }T\}.

A
game strategy
for
\alg
(resp.
\adv
) is a choice of a specific move at every non leaf node corresponding to
\alg
(resp.
\adv
). Thus, any game strategy
Q
for
\alg
(resp. game strategy
A
for
\adv
) corresponds to a subtree of the entire game tree, where we select a single outgoing edge at every node corresponding to
\alg
(resp.
\adv
). Such a subtree is called a
decision tree for
\alg
(resp.
\adv
). With a slight abuse of notation, we will use
v(Q)
(resp.
v(A)
) to denote the values of these decision trees. A simultaneous choice of strategies
Q,A
yields a single path in the original game tree;
v(Q,A)
will denote the value of this path (subtree).

We note that we can express the notion of an
\varepsilon
-hard adversary for
\ell
using this notation; it follows directly from the definition of the value function
v(\cdot)
.

Observation 32
.

An adversary
A
is
\varepsilon
-hard for
\ell
-rounds iff
\inf_{Q}v(Q,A)>\ell
, where the infimum is taken over all strategies for
\alg
.

We now focus on proving Lemma
15
, which requires a few preparatory observations. The first is the following, which is a useful extension of the recursive nature of the value of a subtree to infinite horizon trees.

Lemma 33
.

Let
T
be any subtree of the game tree (not necessarily of finite horizon). Then,

•

If
T
consists of a single node
N
,
v(T)=\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(N)
if
N
is an
\varepsilon
-unambiguous node, else
v(N)=\infty
.

•

If
T
is rooted at a node
N
corresponding to
\alg
, then
v(T)=\inf\{v(T^{\prime}):T^{\prime}\textrm{ child subtree in }T\textrm{ at }N\}
.

•

If
T
is rooted at a node
N
corresponding to
\adv
, then
v(T)=\sup\{v(T^{\prime}):T^{\prime}\textrm{ child subtree in }T\textrm{ at }N\}
.

Proof.

The result follows from the fact that for any finite horizon truncation
T^{\prime}
of
T
, the subtrees of
T^{\prime}
rooted at the children of the root of
T^{\prime}
(and
T
) are finite horizon truncations of the subtrees of
T
rooted at the children of the root.
∎

The following technical property will be used several times in the sequel.

Lemma 34
.

The value of the full game is finite if and only if every full subgame has finite value.

Proof.

Since the full game is a subgame of itself, if every subgame has finite value so does the full game. For the other direction, suppose now that the full game tree has finite value. Then, there is a finite horizon truncation
T
with finite value. The next claim shows that for every full subgame tree, its value can be upper bounded using the value of
T
, so in particular is finite as desired.

Claim 35
.

Let
S
be a full subgame tree, and let
N
denote its root node. Then
v(S)\leq v(T)+depth(N)+1
.

Proof.

Let
D
be the depth of
T
. Consider the truncation
S^{\prime}
of
S
which has depth
D
if
N
is a node corresponding to
\alg
, and depth
D+1
if
N
is a node corresponding to
\adv
. Since
S^{\prime}
is a truncation of
S
, it follows that
v(S)\leq v(S^{\prime})
, and so we only need to show
v(S^{\prime})\leq v(T)+depth(N)+1
.

To prove this bound, consider first the case when
N
is a node corresponding to
\alg
. In this case,
T
is isomorphic to a subtree of
S^{\prime}
, i.e., each node of
T
corresponds to a node in
S^{\prime}
in the natural way: the root of
T
corresponds to the root of
S^{\prime}
, the children of the root of
T
corresponds to (some of) the children of the root of
S^{\prime}
, etc. Observe that the nodes corresponding to
\alg
have the same possibilities for moves in both trees
S^{\prime}
and
T
(in fact, every node of
\alg
in the full game tree has the same choice of moves by definition). However, nodes corresponding to
\adv
have a smaller set of choices in
S^{\prime}
, i.e., children, compared to
T
, because they are deeper in the game tree. Thus, in the inductive definition of
v(S^{\prime})
, a supremum computed at a node in
S^{\prime}
for
\adv
is over a smaller set of choices compared to the corresponding supremum when computing
v(T)
. Finally, the depth (in the full game tree) of any node in
S
is precisely
depth(N)
more than the depth of the corresponding node in
T
. Thus,
v(S^{\prime})\leq v(T)+depth(N)\leq v(T)+depth(N)+1
.

Next, consider the case when
N
is a node corresponding to
\adv
. All the subtrees of
S^{\prime}
rooted at the children of
N
are again in correspondence with
T
, since
S
is of depth
D+1
. The previous argument applies to these subtrees and thus, their values are all bounded by
v(T)+depth(N)+1
. Taking a supremum at node
N
shows that
v(S^{\prime})\leq v(T)+depth(N)+1
.
∎

∎

We first prove the existence of a saddle-point in this game (in fact, the same argument gives that there is a subgame perfect equilibrium, which essentially means that this saddle point property holds for every subtree).

Lemma 36
.

Suppose the value of the full game is finite (in other words, the optimization problem is solvable with finitely many queries using the given oracles). Then, there exists a saddle-point
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
for
\alg
and
\adv
respectively, i.e., strategies
Q^{\star}
and
A^{\star}
satisfying

\displaystyle\inf_{Q}v(Q,A^{\star})=v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})=\sup_{A}\;v(Q^{\star},A)=v(\textup{full game}),

(17)

where
full game
denotes the full game tree, the infimum on the left-hand side is over all possible strategies for
\alg
, and the supremum on the right-hand side is over all strategies for
\adv
.

Proof.

Since we assume the full game has finite value, by Lemma
34
we know that the value
v(S)
of any full subgame tree
S
is also finite. Combined with the fact that
v(S)
is integer valued, the supremum or infimum in Lemma
33
is attained. A saddle-point
Q^{\star}
and
A^{\star}
can now be defined as follows. At any node
N
corresponding to a move by
\alg
, select the move that attains the infimum giving the value of the full subgame tree rooted at
N
by Lemma
33
. At any node
N
corresponding to a move by
\adv
, select the move that attains the supremum giving the value of the full subgame tree rooted at
N
by Lemma
33
.

We now prove that
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
satisfy the desired properties. When the full tree is finite, this follows directly from the definition of
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
; the case where it is not finite is that requires more subtle arguments based on finite horizon truncations. Let
T
denote the full game tree, and for a node
u
let
T_{u}
denote its full subtree rooted at
u
.

We start by proving that
v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})=v(T)
. Let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots
be the nodes in the path
P^{\star}
induced by
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
(
p_{1}
being the root of the full game tree). By the second item of Lemma
33
, we have that
v(T_{p_{1}})
is the smallest value of the subtrees rooted at the children of
p_{1}
; since the strategy
Q^{\star}
chooses precisely such child
p_{2}
of smallest value, we get
v(T_{p_{1}})=v(T_{p_{2}})
. Similarly, using the same lemma and the definition of the adversarial strategy
A^{\star}
, we get
v(T_{p_{2}})=v(T_{p_{3}})
. Thus, subtrees
T_{p_{i}}
’s have the same value, equal to
v(T_{p_{1}})=v(T)
(which is finite). Moreover, the value of a subtree
T_{u}
is at least half of the depth of
u
:
v(T_{u})
is either
\infty
or is the (finite) payoff of a node
w
in
T_{u}
, which is at least half the depth of
w
, and so at least half the depth of
u
. Therefore, all nodes in the path
P^{\star}
are at depth at most
2\cdot v(T)
; in particular,
P^{\star}
has finite length. Now consider a finite horizon truncation
T^{\prime}
of
T
with value
v(T^{\prime})=v(T)
; we can further increase the depth of this truncation and assume that the path
P^{\star}
is contained in
T^{\prime}
.
Since
T^{\prime}
is finite, its value
v(T^{\prime})
is defined by taking the child that has subtree of smallest/largest value on the nodes of
\alg
/
\adv
, until a leaf (of finite value) is reached. But this is by construction the choices that the path
P^{\star}
makes, namely they reach the same leaf (or leaves of the same value). The values of
v(T^{\prime})
and
v(P^{\star})
are then the value of this leaf/leaves, and hence
v(T^{\prime})=v(P^{\star})
, or equivalently
v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})=v(T)
as desired.

We now consider the first desired equation, namely
\inf_{Q}v(Q,A^{\star})=v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})
, and notice that it suffices to show

\displaystyle\inf_{Q}v(Q,A^{\star})\geq v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})

(18)

(the equality follows by taking
Q=Q^{\star}
). Assume that the paths induced by
Q,A^{\star}
and
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
are different, else there is nothing to show. Let
u
be the first node of
\alg
in these paths where
Q
and
Q^{\star}
make different decisions; let
w
and
w^{\star}
be the children of
u
on the paths induced by
Q,A^{\star}
and
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
respectively. Notice that by construction of the choice
Q^{\star}
makes at
u
, we have
v(T_{w^{\star}})\leq v(T_{w})
.

Now we claim that
v(Q,A^{\star})\geq v(T_{w})
. If the path
P
induced by
Q,A^{\star}
has infinite length, then all of its nodes are
\varepsilon
-ambiguous and so by definition
v(Q,A^{\star})=\infty
and the inequality holds. So suppose
P
has finite length. Since the value
v(T_{w})
is finite, again due to our assumption that
v(T)
is finite, there is a finite horizon truncation
T^{\prime}
of this subtree with the same value; again, we can further increase the depth of this truncation and assume that the path
P\cap T_{w}
(the suffix of
P
starting at
w
) is contained in
T^{\prime}
(and hence
P\cap T_{w}=P\cap T^{\prime}
). By definition, the nodes of
\adv
in the path
P\cap T^{\prime}
always select the action that lead to the child whose subtree has largest value (being based on
A^{\star}
), while the nodes of
\alg
may not necessarily select the children with lowest value subtrees (being based on
Q
). Since in the definition of the value
v(T^{\prime})
, both the nodes of
\adv
and
\alg
make the optimal decisions, we see that
v(T^{\prime})\leq v(P\cap T^{\prime})
. Since the value of the path
v(P)
and of its suffix
v(P\cap T^{\prime})
are the same (they are the value of the leaf of the path), we obtain
v(T_{w})=v(T^{\prime})\leq v(P\cap T^{\prime})=v(P)=v(Q,A^{\star})
, as claimed.

Next, we claim that
v(T_{w^{\star}})=v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})
. As proved above, the path
P^{\star}
induced by
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
has finite length. Since the value
v(T_{w^{\star}})
is finite, as above, consider a truncation
T^{\prime}
of
T_{w^{\star}}
with same value
v(T^{\prime})=v(T_{w^{\star}})
and that contains the suffix
P^{\star}\cap T_{w^{\star}}
. By the same argument as in the previous paragraph, but now using that both
Q^{\star}
and
A^{\star}
make optimal decisions, we have
v(T^{\prime})=v(P^{\star}\cap T_{w^{\star}})
, which is also equal to
v(P^{\star})
; this implies
v(T_{w^{\star}})=v(P^{\star})=v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})
as desired.

Putting together the bounds from the three previous paragraphs yields
v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})=v(T_{w^{\star}})\leq v(T_{w})\leq v(Q,A^{\star})
for every strategy
Q
for the algorithm. Taking an infimum over all such
Q
’s finally proves (
18
), and hence that
\inf_{Q}v(Q,A^{\star})=v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})
.

The proof that
\sup_{A}v(Q^{\star},A)=v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})
uses the exact same arguments, but exchanging the roles of the players
\alg
and
\adv
.
∎

By standard arguments, the existence of a saddle-point implies a minimax result, which is the first element required to prove Lemma
15
.

Lemma 37
.

Suppose the value of the full game is finite, and let
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
be a subgame perfect equilibrium guaranteed to exist by Lemma
36
. The the following minimax holds;

\sup_{A}\inf_{Q}\;v(Q,A)=v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})=\inf_{Q}\;\sup_{A}\;v(Q,A),

where the infima are over all possible strategies for
\alg
, and the suprema are over all strategies for
\adv
.

Proof.

From the guarantees of
Q^{\star},A^{\star}
, for all strategies
Q,A
for
\alg
and
\adv
respectively, we have

v(Q^{\star},A^{\star})\leq\inf_{Q}v(Q,A^{\star})\leq\sup_{A}\inf_{Q}v(Q,A)

and

\inf_{Q}\sup_{A}v(Q,A)\leq\sup_{A}v(Q^{\star},A)\leq v(Q^{\star},A^{\star}).

Since we always have
\sup_{A}\inf_{Q}v(Q,A)\leq\inf_{Q}\sup_{A}v(Q,A)
, we get equalities throughout.
∎

The last element required for proving Lemma
15
is the equivalence between the value of this game and the information complexity of the underlying optimization problem.

Lemma 38
.

The value of the full game
v(\textup{full game})
equals
\icomp_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{O})
.

Proof.

Combining Lemmas
36
and
37
,
v(\textup{full game})=\inf_{Q}\sup_{A}v(Q,A)
, so it suffices to show

\inf_{Q}\sup_{A}v(Q,A)=\inf_{Q}\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\icomp\textstyle{{}_{\varepsilon}}(Q,I,\mathcal{O}).

To see the “
\geq
” direction: Consider any strategy
Q
for
\alg
and instance
I\in\mathcal{I}
. Consider the adversary
A
based on the instance
I
, namely that reports
q(I)
whenever
Q
asks a query
q
. Notice that
v(Q,A)=\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})
(if
\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})
is finite, after exactly
\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})
many moves of the algorithm
Q
we reach an
\varepsilon
-unambiguous node in the path induced by
Q,A
, so
v(Q,A)=\icomp_{\textstyle\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})
; if
\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})
is infinite, every finite horizon truncation of the path induced by
Q,A
must end on an
\varepsilon
-ambiguous node, so
v(Q,A)
is also infinite). That is, for every
I
we can find a strategy
A
for the adversary with the “same value”, which then implies
\sup_{A}v(Q,A)\geq\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})
. Taking an infimum over all
Q
’s on both sides gives the desired inequality
\inf_{Q}\sup_{A}v(Q,A)\geq\inf_{Q}\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})
.

The proof for the “
\leq
” direction is analogous: Consider any strategy
Q
for
\alg
and strategy
A
for
\adv
. If
v(Q,A)=\infty
, then for every finite horizon truncation of length
N
(i.e. with
\frac{N}{2}
queries by the algorithm) of the path induced by
Q,A
ends on an
\varepsilon
-ambiguous node; let
I\in\mathcal{I}
be an instance consistent with
A
given up to this node. Then we see that
\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})\geq\frac{N}{2}
. Since this holds for every even number
N
, we get
\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})=\infty
. Similarly, if
v(Q,A)
is finite, then there is a node
L
in the path induced by
Q,A
with
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(L)=v(Q,A)
; any instance
I
associated to this node
L
(i.e., compatible with the answers given by
A
until reaching
L
) then has
\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})\geq\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(L)=v(Q,A)
. These observations imply
\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})\geq\sup_{A}v(Q,A)
. Taking an infimum over
Q
gives
\inf_{Q}\sup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\icomp_{\varepsilon}(Q,I,\mathcal{O})\geq\inf_{Q}\sup_{A}v(Q,A)
as desired.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Now we can finally prove Lemma
15
, stated in the beginning of the section.

Proof of Lemma
15
.

From Observation
32
, there is an adversary
A
that is
\varepsilon
-hard for
\ell
-rounds iff
\sup_{A}\inf_{Q}v(Q,A)>\ell
. Combining Lemmas
36
,
37
, and
38
, the latter is equivalent to
\icomp_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{O})>\ell
, which concludes the proof.
∎
